We close the weak endpoint with a coarser triangular order than in the
strong argument: each step removes the entire highest nonpure homogeneous
class rather than a single flag coordinate.

\begin{proposition}[Local homogeneous-degree reduction]
\label{prop:degree-local-reduction}
Let $P$ be a real polynomial of degree at most $d$ whose highest nonpure
homogeneous block $P_m$ satisfies $\norm{P_m}_{\rm coeff}=1$.  Let
$\Omega\in L^1(\Sph^{n-1})$ and let $f$ be bounded and compactly
supported.  On each unit output cube, the local path
\eqref{eq:strong-local-path} is, modulo pure input and output phases, the
sum of
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item a localized path of the form \eqref{eq:localized-full-path} whose
phase has nonpure total degree at most $m-1$;
\item an error $E$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:degree-local-error}
 V^1(E)(x)\le C_{n,d}
 \int_{|x-y|<1}\frac{|\Omega((x-y)/|x-y|)|}{|x-y|^{n-1}}|f(y)|\,dy.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
The majorant has $L^1$ norm at most
$C_{n,d}\norm\Omega_1\norm f_1$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let the output cube be centered at $a$ and write $x=a+X,y=a+Y$.
Simultaneous translation preserves the degree-$m$ class and creates only
lower degrees.  Remove all pure terms and let $H_m$ be the mixed
representative of the normalized top class.  Replace it by the anchored
representative
\[
 \widehat H_m(X,Y)=H_m(X,Y)-H_m(Y,Y),
\]
which differs by a pure input phase and satisfies
$\widehat H_m(Y,Y)=0$.  On the fixed bounded set relevant to
$|X-Y|<1$,
\[
 |\widehat H_m(X,Y)|
 \le |X-Y|\int_0^1
 |\partial_xH_m(Y+t(X-Y),Y)|\,dt
 \le C_{n,d}|X-Y|.
\]
The last constant is uniform over the compact unit sphere of
$\mathcal V_m$.  Replacing $e^{i\widehat H_m}$ by one therefore creates
exactly the total-variation majorant \eqref{eq:degree-local-error}.  What
remains has nonpure degree at most $m-1$.  Young's inequality proves the
$L^1$ assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Weak polynomial critical jumps on core data]
\label{prop:weak-polynomial-jump}
For every real polynomial $P$ of degree at most $d$, every mean-zero
$\Omega\in L\log L(\Sph^{n-1})$, and every bounded compactly supported
$f$,
\[
 \sup_{\lambda>0}
 \norm{\lambda N_\lambda(T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f:
 \varepsilon>0)^{1/2}}_{L^{1,\infty}}
 \le C_{n,d}\mathfrak L(\Omega)\norm f_1,
\]
with a constant independent of the coefficients of $P$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Induct on the largest total degree $m$ carrying a nonpure homogeneous
class.  If there is no such class, the phase is pure and
Proposition~\ref{prop:nonoscweak} applies after input and output
conjugation.  Assume the assertion for nonpure degree at most $m-1$ and
let
\[
 C_m=\norm{P_m}_{\rm coeff}>0,
 \qquad \rho=C_m^{-1/m}.
\]
Under $x=\rho X,y=\rho Y$, the entire degree-$m$ class, rather than one
scalar coordinate, has norm one.  Proposition~\ref{prop:farweak} controls
the normalized far path.

For the local path use Proposition~\ref{prop:degree-local-reduction}.
The error is strong $(1,1)$ in total variation by
\eqref{eq:degree-local-error}.  For the residual phase, subtracting the
fixed truncation at one does not change increments below $1$; adjoining
zero for $\varepsilon\ge1$ creates no additional jump beyond the precise
domination \eqref{eq:localized-jump-domination}.  Apply the induction hypothesis to
$f$ restricted to the fixed enlargement of each output cube.  The output
cubes are disjoint and the input enlargements have bounded overlap, so
their weak distribution bounds sum to
$C\alpha^{-1}\mathfrak L(\Omega)\norm f_1$.

Combine local and far paths with \eqref{eq:jumpadd}.  Finally the exact
conjugacy
\[
 T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f(\rho X)
 =T^{P_\rho}_{\Omega,\varepsilon/\rho}f_\rho(X),
 \qquad f_\rho(X)=f(\rho X),
\]
makes both input and weak output $L^1$ norms acquire the same factor
$\rho^{-n}$.  Thus the bound is independent of $C_m$.  The finite degree
induction proves the proposition for every polynomial phase.
\end{proof}

\section{Completion, consequences, and radial
\texorpdfstring{$\BV$}{BV} transfer}\label{sec:limits}

\subsection{Completion of the critical-jump trajectory}

For core data, the weak endpoint propositions already remove the
auxiliary cube, shell, and rational-parameter cutoffs.  We pass to
general $L^1$ data and construct the complete trajectory over all positive
truncation parameters.

For bounded compactly supported $f$, finite annular differences are
absolutely convergent.  On each compact interval $0<a<b<\infty$,
\[
 \int_{a<|z|<b}|K_\Omega(z)|\,|f(x-z)|dz<\infty
\]
for almost every $x$ by Fubini.  Hence the truncation path is continuous in
$\varepsilon$ on compact subintervals of $(0,\infty)$ for almost every
$x$.  Jumps over positive rationals therefore equal jumps over all positive
parameters.

For such core data the path is zero for sufficiently large truncation at
each fixed $x$.  Thus the jump estimate implies the maximal estimate
\begin{equation}\label{eq:maximalfromjump}
 \norm{\sup_{q\in\mathbb Q_+}|T^P_{\Omega,q}h|}_{L^{1,\infty}}
 \le C_{n,d}\mathfrak L(\Omega)\norm h_1.
\end{equation}
Indeed, if $|T^P_{\Omega,q}h|>\tau$, compare it with a sufficiently large rational truncation
where the value is zero and use one strict $\tau/2$-jump.

Let $f_k$ be bounded compactly supported functions with
$f_k\to f$ in $L^1$ and
$\norm{f_{k+1}-f_k}_1\le2^{-3k}$.  By
\eqref{eq:maximalfromjump},
\[
 \sum_k\left|\left\{
 \sup_{q\in\mathbb Q_+}|T^P_{\Omega,q}(f_{k+1}-f_k)|>2^{-k}\right\}\right|<\infty.
\]
Borel--Cantelli gives almost-everywhere uniform convergence on
$\mathbb Q_+$.  This defines $T^P_{\Omega,q}f$ simultaneously for all
rational $q>0$.  More explicitly, outside the Borel--Cantelli exceptional
set there is $k_0(x)$ such that, for every $k\ge k_0(x)$,
\[
 \sup_{q\in\mathbb Q_+}
 |T^P_{\Omega,q}(f_{k+1}-f_k)(x)|\le2^{-k}.
\]
Consequently, for every $m\ge k_0(x)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rational-uniform-tail}
 \sup_{q\in\mathbb Q_+}
 |T^P_{\Omega,q}(f-f_m)(x)|
 \le\sum_{k\ge m}2^{-k}.
\end{equation}
For the core function $f_m$, the trajectory is zero at $x$ for all
sufficiently large $q$.  Taking the upper limit as $q\to\infty$ in
\eqref{eq:rational-uniform-tail} and then letting $m\to\infty$ proves the
rational zero-at-infinity anchor.
After passing to a further subsequence, Young's inequality for the
finite-annulus kernels and a diagonal argument ensure, on the same
full-measure set, convergence of the finite-annulus identities for every
rational pair of endpoints.  Define the value at arbitrary $\varepsilon$
from the base point $1$ by
\[
 T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f=T^P_{\Omega,1}f+
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\int_{\varepsilon<|z|\le1}e^{iP(x,x-z)}K_\Omega(z)f(x-z)dz,
 &\varepsilon<1,\\[1ex]
 \displaystyle-\int_{1<|z|\le\varepsilon}e^{iP(x,x-z)}K_\Omega(z)f(x-z)dz,
 &\varepsilon>1.
 \end{cases}
\]
The added kernels are $L^1$ on every finite annulus, so the definition is
valid almost everywhere and locally continuous in $\varepsilon$.  It
agrees with the rational limits.

For every finite rational parameter set, Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-witness}
and Fatou's lemma transfer the distribution estimate to $f$.  Increasing
finite rational sets, followed by local continuity, gives all positive
truncation parameters.  The rational anchor just proved and the
finite-annulus definition show that the path tends to zero at infinity
over all positive radii.  Two paths with the same finite-shell increments differ by a
function independent of $\varepsilon$; the zero anchor therefore makes this
completion unique.  This completes the unweighted weak critical-jump
construction and, together with the strong theorem already proved,
establishes Theorem~\ref{thm:main}.

\subsection{Abstract consequences}

The strong variation estimate is
\eqref{eq:strong-polynomial-variation}.  At the endpoint,
apply \eqref{eq:weak-jump-to-var} first on an increasing sequence of
finite ordered subsets of $\mathbb Q_+$.  Its bound is uniform in the
finite set.  Monotonicity of $V^r$ under enlargement of the parameter set
and Fatou's lemma for distribution functions pass the estimate to
$\mathbb Q_+$.  Local continuity and Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-witness} then
recover all positive parameters.  The unweighted weak jump theorem gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:unweighted-weak-var}
 \norm{V^r(T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f:\varepsilon>0)}_{L^{1,\infty}}
 \le C_{n,d}\frac r{r-2}\mathfrak L(\Omega)\norm f_1.
\end{equation}
The constants remain coefficient-uniform because both abstract
implications are uniform over the parameter set.  The zero anchor gives the
maximal estimate, and finite $r$-variation gives the limit at zero.  This
proves Corollary~\ref{cor:unweighted-consequences}.

\subsection{Radial \texorpdfstring{$\BV$}{BV} transfer}

Write
\[
 F_\varepsilon=T^P_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f,
 \qquad G_\varepsilon=T^{P,b}_{\Omega,\varepsilon}f.
\]
For test data, Stieltjes integration by parts on a finite interval gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stieltjes-finite}
 G_\varepsilon-G_R
 =b(\varepsilon)F_\varepsilon-b(R)F_R
   +\int_{(\varepsilon,R]}F_s\,db(s).
\end{equation}
Here $db((s,t])=b(t)-b(s)$ is the Stieltjes measure associated with the
fixed right-continuous representative of $b$.  Altering $b$ at a single
radius does not change the original Lebesgue shell integral, whereas the
Stieltjes formula depends on the chosen representative.  We next justify
the passage to general data on one common full-measure set.

Use the representatives of $F_\varepsilon$ constructed in the strong
completion and in the preceding weak endpoint completion.  In both cases
the base coordinate $F_1$ is the almost-everywhere limit of core-data
coordinates, and every other coordinate is fixed by its signed
finite-annulus increment from $1$.  Choose the corresponding core
approximation $f_k$, sufficiently rapidly that the maximal norms of its
successive differences are summable.  The maximal estimates used in those
constructions then allow a subsequence for which $F^{(k)}_q$ converges uniformly in
$q\in\mathbb Q_+$ outside one null set.  Since every core-data difference
is locally continuous in the truncation parameter, the same subsequence is
uniformly Cauchy on each compact parameter interval: for a continuous
difference, the supremum on such an interval is its supremum on the
rational parameters there.

For every rational pair $0<a<c<\infty$, Young's inequality for
\begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-annulus-L1}
 \norm{K_\Omega\mathbf1_{\{a<|\cdot|\le c\}}}_1
 \le\norm\Omega_1\log(c/a)
\end{equation}
passes the finite-annulus identity to the limit.  A diagonal subsequence
and countability leave one full-measure set on which all rational
identities and the convergence of the anchored base coordinate hold
simultaneously.  Enlarge its null complement by the Fubini null sets on
$\{2^{-j}<|z|<2^j\}$, $j\ge1$, and by the null sets on which the zero
anchor or finite $3$-variation fails.  On the resulting common
full-measure set,
\[
 F_R\longrightarrow0\quad(R\to\infty),
 \qquad M_F:=\sup_{s>0}|F_s|<\infty,
\]
and $F$ is locally absolutely continuous in the truncation parameter.
Indeed, with
\[
 \mathcal A_x(r)=\int_{\Sph^{n-1}}e^{iP(x,x-r\theta)}
       \Omega(\theta)f(x-r\theta)\,d\sigma(\theta),
\]
Fubini locally in $x$ (using H\"older's inequality on bounded $x$-sets
when $f\in L^p$) gives, for every compact $0<a<c<\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:radial-density}
 \int_a^c|\mathcal A_x(r)|\,\frac{dr}{r}<\infty,
 \qquad
 F_a(x)-F_c(x)=\int_a^c\mathcal A_x(r)\,\frac{dr}{r}.
\end{equation}
The rational identities and absolute continuity give
\eqref{eq:radial-density} for all real $0<a<c<\infty$.

On this same set define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stieltjes}
 G_\varepsilon=b(\varepsilon)F_\varepsilon
 +\int_{(\varepsilon,\infty)}F_s\,db(s).
\end{equation}
The Stieltjes integral is absolutely convergent because
$|db|(0,\infty)<\infty$ and $M_F<\infty$.  For
$0<\varepsilon<R<\infty$, integration by parts for the locally absolutely
continuous path $F$ and the fixed right-continuous representative of $b$
uses the product formula
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stieltjes-product-rule}
 b(R)F_R-b(\varepsilon)F_\varepsilon
 =\int_{(\varepsilon,R]}F_s\,db(s)
  +\int_\varepsilon^R b(s)\,dF_s.
\end{equation}
There is no ambiguity between $b(s)$ and $b(s-)$ in the second integral,
because $dF$ has no atoms.  By \eqref{eq:radial-density},
\[
 dF_s=-\mathcal A_x(s)\,\frac{ds}{s}.
\]
Consequently, \eqref{eq:stieltjes-product-rule} gives
\begin{align}
 G_\varepsilon-G_R
 &=b(\varepsilon)F_\varepsilon-b(R)F_R
   +\int_{(\varepsilon,R]}F_s\,db(s)\notag\\
 &=-\int_{(\varepsilon,R]}b(s)\,dF_s\notag\\
 &=\int_{\varepsilon<|z|\le R}
   e^{iP(x,x-z)}K_\Omega(z)b(|z|)f(x-z)\,dz.
 \label{eq:general-bv-finite-shell}
\end{align}
Indeed,
$-\int_\varepsilon^R b(s)\,dF_s
=\int_\varepsilon^R b(s)\mathcal A_x(s)\,ds/s$, and polar coordinates
give exactly the last line, including its sign and half-open endpoint
convention.
The last integral is absolutely meaningful on the same set; its kernel has
$L^1$ norm at most
$\norm b_\infty\norm\Omega_1\log(R/\varepsilon)$.  Hence
\eqref{eq:stieltjes} has exactly the required finite-annulus increments for
general data.  Right continuity fixes the Stieltjes representative, while
altering $b$ at one radius does not change the last integral.  Finally,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bv-anchor-explicit}
 |G_\varepsilon|
 \le\norm b_\infty|F_\varepsilon|
   +M_F|db|((\varepsilon,\infty))\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(\varepsilon\to\infty),
\end{equation}
so the zero anchor uniquely determines the extension.

Put $B=\norm b_\infty$, $V=\Var(b)$, and
$M_F=\sup_{s>0}|F_s|$.  The zero anchor gives
$M_F\le V^r(F)$ for every $r\ge1$.  Product variation and
\eqref{eq:stieltjes} give, pointwise,
\begin{align}
 V^r(bF)&\le B V^r(F)+VM_F,\notag\\
 V^r\left(\int_{(\varepsilon,\infty)}F_s\,db(s):\varepsilon>0\right)
 &\le VM_F,\notag\\
 V^r(G)&\le(B+2V)V^r(F).\label{eq:bv-variation}
\end{align}
Indeed, the second line follows by summing the $|db|$-masses of the
disjoint parameter intervals in a variation partition.

We record separately the critical-jump transfer.  If $M_F=0$, then
$F_s=0$ for every $s>0$, and \eqref{eq:stieltjes} gives $G_\varepsilon=0$;
all assertions are then immediate.  Hence assume $M_F>0$.  Suppose first
that $B>0$.  For an ordered family of $\lambda$-jumps of $bF$, write
\[
 b(t)F_t-b(s)F_s
 =b(t)(F_t-F_s)+(b(t)-b(s))F_s.
\]
Assign the pair to the first term if its modulus is $>\lambda/2$, and
otherwise to the second.  The first subfamily contributes at most
$N_{\lambda/(2B)}(F)$.  The parameter intervals in the second subfamily
are disjoint, and hence
\[
 \frac{\lambda}{2M_F}N_{\rm second}
 <\sum_{\rm second}|b(t)-b(s)|\le V.
\]
This proves
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bF-count}
 N_\lambda(bF)
 \le N_{\lambda/(2B)}(F)+\frac{2M_FV}{\lambda}.
\end{equation}
On the set where the left side is nonzero, $\lambda\le2BM_F$.  With
$\mu=\lambda/(2B)$, the second term after taking square roots is bounded
by
\[
 \sqrt{2\lambda M_FV}
 \le2M_F\sqrt{BV}\le(B+V)M_F.
\]
It follows that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bF-jump}
 \lambda N_\lambda(bF)^{1/2}
 \le2B\mu N_\mu(F)^{1/2}+C(B+V)M_F.
\end{equation}
For $B=0$ the path is zero.  Define the Stieltjes-tail path by
\[
 H_\varepsilon=\int_{(\varepsilon,\infty)}F_s\,db(s).
\]
It satisfies $V^1(H)\le VM_F$; since a nonzero
$\lambda$-jump of $H$ also forces $\lambda\le2VM_F$, while the disjoint
jump intervals give $N_\lambda(H)\le VM_F/\lambda$.  Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tail-jump}
 \lambda N_\lambda(H)^{1/2}\le C VM_F.
\end{equation}
Taking the supremum in $\lambda$ after applying
\eqref{eq:jumpadd}, \eqref{eq:bF-jump}, and \eqref{eq:tail-jump}
proves the critical-jump bounds for $G$.  In strong $L^p$ use
\eqref{eq:strong-polynomial-variation} with, say, $r=3$ to control $M_F$; in
weak $L^1$ use \eqref{eq:unweighted-weak-var} with $r=3$ and the
quasi-triangle inequality in $L^{1,\infty}$.  Formula
\eqref{eq:bv-variation} gives the asserted strong and weak $r$-variation
bounds.  This proves the estimates in Corollary~\ref{cor:bv-extension}.

The almost-everywhere zero anchor was proved in
\eqref{eq:bv-anchor-explicit}; for $f\in L^p$, both terms there also tend
to zero in $L^p$.  Since a scalar path of finite $r$-variation has
one-sided limits at both endpoints, $G_\varepsilon$ has an almost-everywhere
limit as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$.  The zero anchor gives
$\sup_{\varepsilon>0}|G_\varepsilon|\le V^r(G)$ and hence the maximal
truncation assertion.  This completes the proof of
Corollary~\ref{cor:bv-extension}.
